A High-Dimensional Statistical Theory for Convex and Nonconvex Matrix Sensing
Joshua Agterberg, René Vidal

Abstract
The problem of matrix sensing, or trace regression, is a problem wherein one wishes to estimate a low-rank matrix from linear measurements perturbed with noise. A number of existing works have studied both convex and nonconvex approaches to this problem, establishing minimax error rates when the number of measurements is sufficiently large relative to the rank and dimension of the low-rank matrix, though a precise comparison of these procedures still remains unexplored. In this work we provide a high-dimensional statistical analysis for symmetric low-rank matrix sensing observed under Gaussian measurements and noise. Our main result describes a novel phenomenon: in this statistical model and in an appropriate asymptotic regime, the behavior of any local minimum of the nonconvex factorized approach (with known rank) is approximately equivalent to that of the matrix hard-thresholding of a corresponding matrix denoising problem, and the behavior of the convex nuclear-norm regularized least squares approach is approximately equivalent to that of matrix soft-thresholding of the same matrix denoising problem. Here “approximately equivalent” is understood in the sense of concentration of Lipchitz functions. As a consequence, the nonconvex procedure uniformly dominates the convex approach in mean squared error. Our arguments are based on a matrix operator generalization of the Convex Gaussian Min-Max Theorem (CGMT) together with studying the interplay between local minima of the convex and nonconvex formulations and their “debiased” counterparts, and several of these results may be of independent interest.



Introduction

In the
matrix sensing
or
trace regression
problem, the goal is to estimate a symmetric matrix
\bm{M}
from
n
observations
(y_{i},\bm{X}_{i})\in\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}^{d\times d}
such that

\displaystyle y_{i}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\langle\bm{X}_{i},\bm{M}\rangle+\varepsilon_{i},

(1)

where
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle
denotes the Frobenius inner product,
\varepsilon_{i}
is observation noise, and
\bm{M}
is assumed to be a symmetric positive semidefinite rank
r
signal matrix of interest with
r
typically taken to be much smaller than the dimension
d
(the scaling by
\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}
is primarily for theoretical purposes). Equation (
1
) serves as a simple-but-practical model for high-dimensional matrix regression, and this model has drawn attention from fields such as computer science, engineering, and statistics. This problem can be viewed as a matrix generalization of the noisy sparse recovery problem, in which one instead observes linear measurements of a sparse signal vector perturbed by random noise, with the rank of the signal matrix
\bm{M}
playing the role of sparsity.

To address the low-rank structure of the signal matrix, drawing inspiration from the compressed sensing literature, initial work focused on a penalized least-squares approach to estimation, often referred to as the Matrix LASSO.
Explicitly, the Matrix LASSO with tuning parameter
\lambda
, denoted
\bm{Z}^{(\lambda)}
, solves the problem

\displaystyle\bm{Z}^{(\lambda)}=\argmin_{\bm{Z}\succcurlyeq 0}g_{\sf cvx}^{(\lambda)}(\bm{Z}):=\frac{1}{2}\sum_{i=1}^{n}(y_{i}-\langle\bm{X}_{i},\bm{Z}\rangle/\sqrt{n})^{2}+\lambda\|\bm{Z}\|_{*},

(2)

where
\|\cdot\|_{*}
denotes the nuclear norm on
d\times d
matrices (here we take any minimizer of the objective function).
The right-hand side is convex in the optimization variable
\bm{Z}
, hence the minimizer is computable in polynomial time. Much work has been devoted to the study of
\bm{Z}^{(\lambda)}
, including conditions on the sensing matrices
\bm{X}_{i}
and the tuning parameter
\lambda
such that approximate recovery is possible. As a representative example, the work of
Candès and Plan 2011
proved that for Gaussian observations
\{\bm{X}_{i}\}_{i=1}^{n}
, when
n\geq C_{0}dr
and
\varepsilon_{i}\sim\mathcal{N}(0,\sigma^{2})
, it holds that

\displaystyle\|\bm{Z}^{(\lambda)}-\bm{M}\|_{F}^{2}\leq C\sigma^{2}dr

(3)

with high probability as long as
\lambda
is taken to be
C^{\prime}\sigma\sqrt{d}
, where
C,C^{\prime},
and
C_{0}
are some universal constants. Moreover,
Candès and Plan 2011
show that this procedure is minimax rate-optimal in the sense that no estimator can improve the order of the right hand side of Equation (
3
).

While the estimator
\bm{Z}^{(\lambda)}
is computable in polynomial time, computing it in practice often requires running an iterative algorithm such as projected gradient descent. A major drawback to projected gradient descent and its variants is that each iteration requires taking a (full) singular value decomposition of a
d\times d
matrix, which can be prohibitive when
d
is large. To ameliorate the need for multiple full SVDs, many researchers have instead proposed using a pre-factorized version of the problem that operates directly on the factors of the matrix, often referred to as the “Burer-Monteiro” factorization from the work of
Burer and Monteiro 2003
. Explicitly, given a pre-specified rank
r
, the nonconvex factorized estimator
\bm{U}^{(\lambda)}\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times r}
solves the problem

\displaystyle\bm{U}^{(\lambda)}=\argmin_{\bm{U}\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times r}}f_{{\sf ncvx}}^{(\lambda)}(\bm{U}):=\frac{1}{4}\sum_{i=1}^{n}(y_{i}-\langle\bm{X}_{i},\bm{UU}^{\top}\rangle/\sqrt{n})^{2}+\frac{\lambda}{2}\|\bm{U}\|_{F}^{2}.

(4)

The right hand side of (
4
) is nonconvex in the optimization variable
\bm{U}
, but iterative procedures such as gradient descent have the benefit of operating directly on the low-rank factors, thereby reducing the computational burden when
r
is small relative to
d
. In this paper we are interested both in
\lambda>0
but also in the special case where
\lambda=0
, which is often used in practice.

Existing work has demonstrated that despite introducing nonconvexity, the particular form of nonconvexity is “harmless” in the sense that the optimization landscape is benign (in the noiseless setting)
(
Haeffele et al. 2014
;
Bhojanapalli et al. 2016
;
Li et al. 2019
;
Ma and Fattahi 2023
)
, or that local optima exhibit minimax-optimal statistical guarantees similar to existing results for the convex estimator
\bm{Z}^{(\lambda)}

(
Ge et al. 2017
;
Negahban and Wainwright 2011
;
Tu et al. 2016
;
Zheng and Lafferty 2015
)
. See
Section

1.5
for more details.

Despite these advancements, a more comprehensive statistical theory is still missing from the literature.
To draw an analogy, in the statistical literature on high-dimensional linear regression,
a precise asymptotic statistical theory for the LASSO estimator has emerged over a series of works
(
Bayati and Montanari 2012
;
Javanmard and Montanari 2014a
;
Javanmard and Montanari 2018
;
Bellec and Zhang 2023
;
Celentano et al. 2023
;
Miolane and Montanari 2021
;
Thrampoulidis et al. 2018
;
Thrampoulidis et al. 2015b
)
.
Therefore, partially motivated by these findings, in this paper we are interested in answering the following question:

What is the asymptotic behavior of
\bm{Z}^{(\lambda)}
and
\bm{U}^{(0)}
when
d^{2}\gg n\gg d
with
r
held fixed?

It is worth noting that in our main results we will allow
r
to grow with
n
so that
n\gg dr^{2}
. We deliberately focus on the regime
d^{2}\gg n\gg d
since if
n\gg d^{2}
, then we are once again in the “low-dimensional” regime where least squares is consistent without any additional regularization.

The key takeaway from this work can be stated informally as follows:

The convex estimator
\bm{Z}^{(\lambda)}
behaves asymptotically equivalently to matrix soft thresholding, and the nonconvex estimator
\bm{U}^{(0)}
behaves asymptotically equivalently to matrix hard thresholding, where both thresholding procedures are computed on a noisy version of
\bm{M}
.

In the subsequent sections we formalize what we mean by “behaves asymptotically equivalently to.” Importantly, this asymptotic equivalence also implies a form of statistical dominance: namely, the nonconvex estimator has a mean-squared error strictly smaller than the convex estimator.

Notation

For a veector
\bm{v}
,
\|\bm{v}\|
denotes its Euclidean
\ell_{2}
norm, and for a matrix
\bm{X}
,
\|\bm{X}\|,\|\bm{X}\|_{*}
and
\|\bm{X}\|_{F}
denote its spectral, nuclear, and Frobenius norm, respectively. We write
{\sf vec}(\bm{X})
to denote the (half) vectorization of a (symmetric) matrix. We write
\lambda_{i}(\bm{X})
to denote the
i
’th largest eigenvalue of a matrix, and we let
\lambda_{\min}(\bm{X})
denote its smallest nonzero eigenvalue. For a general function
f:\mathbb{R}^{d\times r}\to\mathbb{R}
we let
\nabla f
denote its gradient, viewed as an element of
\mathbb{R}^{d\times r}
and
\nabla^{2}
denote its Hessian, viewed as an operator on
\mathbb{R}^{d\times r}\times\mathbb{R}^{d\times r}
. For two matrices
\bm{U}
and
\bm{U}^{\prime}
of dimension
d\times r
, we denote
\mathcal{O}_{\bm{U},\bm{U}^{\prime}}
via

\displaystyle\mathcal{O}_{\bm{U},\bm{U}^{\prime}}

\displaystyle=\argmin_{\mathcal{OO}^{\top}=\bm{I}_{r}}\|\bm{U}\mathcal{O}-\bm{U}^{\prime}\|_{F}.

(5)

Throughout we let
C
or
c
denote a universal constant that may change from line to line. For two sequences
a_{n}
and
b_{n}
, we say
a_{n}\ll b_{n}
if
a_{n}/b_{n}\to 0
as
n\to\infty
, and we say
a_{n}\lesssim b_{n}
if
a_{n}\leq Cb_{n}
. We also write
a_{n}=O(b_{n})
if for all
n
sufficiently large
a_{n}\leq Cb_{n}
. Similarly, we write
a_{n}=o(b_{n})
if
a_{n}\ll b_{n}
, and we write
a_{n}=\widetilde{O}(b_{n})
if there exists some constant
c>0
such that
a_{n}=O(\log^{c}(n)b_{n})
.

We write
\mathcal{N}(\mu,\sigma^{2})
to denote the normal distribution with mean
\mu
and variance
\sigma^{2}
. We say a matrix
\bm{X}\sim{\sf GOE}(d)
if
\bm{X}\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times d}
is symmetric with independent
\mathcal{N}(0,1)
entries on the diagonal and independent
\mathcal{N}(0,1/2)
entries on the off-diagonal.
We let
\mathcal{X}:\mathbb{R}^{d\times d}\to\mathbb{R}^{n}
be the operator with elements defined via
\mathcal{X}(\bm{M})_{i}=\langle\bm{X}_{i},\bm{M}\rangle/\sqrt{n}
, and we let
\mathcal{X}^{*}
denote its adjoint. Throughout the rest of the paper, probabilities and expectations without adornment hold according to the randomness in
\{\bm{X}_{i},\varepsilon_{i}\}_{i=1}^{n}
.

We let
L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d\times d};\mathbb{R}^{d\times d})
denote the space of symmetric matrix-valued functions; for two matrix-valued functions
\bm{Q}_{1}(\cdot),\bm{Q}_{2}(\cdot):\mathbb{R}^{d\times d}\to\mathbb{R}^{d\times d}
, we define the inner product

\displaystyle\langle\bm{Q}_{1},\bm{Q}_{2}\rangle_{L_{2},F}:=\int\langle\bm{Q}_{1}(\bm{G}),\bm{Q}_{2}(\bm{G})\rangle d\mathbb{P}(\bm{G}),

(6)

where
\mathbb{P}(\bm{G})
denotes the density with respect to the Gaussian Orthogonal Ensemble. We let the caligraphic letter
\mathcal{E}
denote events.

Preliminaries and Technical Assumptions

Suppose we observe
\{y_{i},\bm{X}_{i}\}_{i=1}^{n}
of the form in (
1
), where
(\varepsilon_{i},\bm{X}_{i})\in\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}^{d\times d}
are such that

\displaystyle\qquad\bm{X}_{i}\sim{\sf GOE}(d),\quad\text{and}\quad\varepsilon_{i}\sim\mathcal{N}(0,\sigma^{2}).

(7)

Here we recall that
\bm{X}_{i}\sim{\sf GOE}(d)
means that
\bm{X}_{i}
is a
d\times d
symmetric matrix with IID
\mathcal{N}(0,1)
entries on the diagonal and IID
\mathcal{N}(0,1/2)
entries on the off-diagonal, referred to as the
Gaussian orthogonal ensemble
.

While these assumptions on
\bm{X}_{i}
and
\varepsilon_{i}
may seem overly idealized, we may view such assumptions as making a “tradeoff” in model specificity and result specificity, with more precise results (typically) requiring stronger modeling assumptions. Moreover, while we assume Gaussianity throughout this work, a series of works in high-dimensional regression suggest that these assumptions can be generalized to other distributions on
\bm{X}_{i}
(e.g., independent entries) via notions of
universality

(
Dudeja et al. 2024
;
Dudeja et al. 2023
;
Han 2024
;
Han and Shen 2023
;
Montanari et al. 2023
)
.
Indeed, the works
Dudeja et al. 2024
;
Dudeja et al. 2023
suggest that many existing results continue to hold only requiring certain asymptotic spectral properties of the observations.
Therefore, while perhaps the statistical model studied herein may seem limited, it is likely that the insights gleaned are not.

Our results relate
\bm{U}^{(\lambda)},\bm{Z}^{(\lambda)}
, and
\bm{U}^{(0)}
to a certain corresponding matrix denoising problem. Suppose one observes
\bm{M}+\sigma\bm{H}
, where
\bm{H}\sim{{\sf GOE}}(d)
.
Define

\displaystyle\bm{Z}_{{\sf ST}}^{(\lambda)}:=\argmin_{\bm{Z}}\frac{1}{2}\|\bm{Z}-\bm{M}-\sigma\bm{H}\|_{F}^{2}+\lambda\|\bm{Z}\|_{*}.

(8)

Equivalently,
\bm{Z}_{{\sf ST}}^{(\lambda)}
is the
matrix soft-thresholding
of
\bm{M}+\sigma\bm{H}
at level
\lambda
, which can be computed as follows. First, suppose that
\bm{M}+\sigma\bm{H}
has SVD
\bm{\widehat{V}}\bm{\widehat{\Lambda}}\bm{\widehat{W}}^{\top}
. Then
\bm{Z}_{{\sf ST}}^{(\lambda)}=\bm{\widehat{V}}\big(\bm{\widehat{\Lambda}}-\lambda\bm{I}\big)_{+}\bm{\widehat{W}}^{\top}
, where for a diagonal matrix
\bm{D}
,
(\bm{D})_{+}
sets the negative entries to zero. In other words,
\bm{Z}_{{\sf ST}}^{(\lambda)}
subtracts
\lambda
from all the singular values of
\bm{M}+\sigma\bm{H}
and sets the negative singular values to zero.

We also define the matrix denoising hard thresholding counterpart

\displaystyle\bm{Z}_{{\sf HT}}:=\mathcal{P}_{{\sf rank}-r}\big(\bm{M}+\sigma\bm{H}\big),

(9)

where
\mathcal{P}_{{\sf rank}-r}
simply computes the best rank
r
approximation to
\bm{M}+\sigma\bm{H}
, which, by the Ekhart-Young Theorem, is equivalent to setting the
d-r
smallest singular values of
\bm{M}+\sigma\bm{H}
to zero. We emphasize that the quantities
\bm{Z}_{{\sf HT}}
and
\bm{Z}_{{\sf ST}}^{(\lambda)}
serve only as theoretical tools. Throughout this work we assume that
\bm{Z}_{{\sf HT}}
is
always
computed assuming that
r
is known, so there is no ambiguity in its definition.

In order to state our results we must impose some assumptions.
Our first assumption concerns the signal strength of the matrix
\bm{M}
.

Assumption 1

(Signal Strength)
.

The low-rank matrix
\bm{M}
satisfies
\bm{M}=\sqrt{d}\bm{V\Lambda V}^{\top}
, where
\bm{\Lambda}
is a diagonal
r\times r
matrix of positive eigenvalues of
\bm{M}
and
\bm{V}
is a
d\times r
orthonormal matrix of corresponding eigenvectors.
The matrix
\bm{\Lambda}
has smallest eigenvalue
\lambda_{r}
that satisfies

\displaystyle C_{1}\sqrt{r}<\lambda_{r}/\sigma<C_{2}\sqrt{r},

(10)

where
C_{1}
and
C_{2}
are some sufficiently large constants. Furthermore, it holds that
\frac{\|\bm{M}\|}{\lambda_{r}\sqrt{d}}\leq\kappa
for some
\kappa=O(1)
. Finally, it holds that
\sigma\geq\sigma_{\min}>c>0
.

If
\|\bm{M}\|
is too small relative to
\sigma
, then the zero matrix achieves the optimal error rate, and hence
1
ensures the problem is well-defined. Furthermore, from classical results in random matrix theory, when
r
is fixed it is known that the leading eigenvectors of
\bm{M}+\sigma\bm{H}
are asymptotically nontrivial estimators of
\bm{V}
when
\lambda_{r}/\sigma>1

(
Benaych-Georges and Nadakuditi 2011
)
. Similarly, by the lower bounds of
Cai and Zhang 2018
, the condition
\lambda_{r}/\sigma\geq C\sqrt{r}
is necessary for minimax consistency of the leading eigenspace of
\bm{M}
. Therefore, our assumption
\lambda_{r}/\sigma>C_{1}\sqrt{r}
, is nearly optimal to ensure that the matrix denoising problem
\bm{M}+\sigma\bm{H}
admits nontrivial estimators for its eigenspace.
Finally, we assume that
\sigma
is bounded below for technical convenience. In principle, our results can be modified to admit the regime where
\sigma\to 0
, but the statements of the results will change, as the probabilities may depend on
\sigma
. Similarly we assume that
\frac{\lambda_{r}}{\sigma}\leq C_{2}\sqrt{r}
for some large constant
C_{2}
, which is only a technical assumption to ensure that the signal to noise ratio (the ratio
\frac{\lambda_{r}}{\sigma\sqrt{r}}
) is of constant order. This assumption is not strong, and is likely a technical artifact, as a diverging SNR only renders the problem simpler from a statistical perspective. This regime mimics existing results for the LASSO in the sub-linear sparsity regime; see the discussion after
Theorem

1
.

Our second assumption concerns the dimensionality of the problem and ensures we are given sufficiently many measurements.

Assumption 2

(Sample Size and Dimension)
.

It holds that
\gamma_{n}:=\frac{n}{dr}
satisfies
\gamma_{n}\geq C_{3}r
, where
C_{3}
is some sufficiently large constant (possibly depending on
C_{1}
,
C_{2}
, and
\kappa
).

From the minimax bounds of
Candès and Plan 2011
, minimax consistency requires
\gamma_{n}\to\infty
, and hence such an assumption is only suboptimal by a factor of
r
. However, the assumption
n\geq Cdr^{2}
is pervasive in the literature studying gradient descent and its variants on the factorized problem. More specifically, such a condition is often required to ensure that the spectral initialization for gradient descent lies within the so-called
basin of attraction
, and it is an open problem to determine whether there is an initialization for gradient descent that converges under the weaker condition
n\geq Cdr
. Consequently,
2
is not particularly strong.

Our third assumption concerns the choice of the regularization parameter
\lambda
in the convex estimator
\bm{Z}^{(\lambda)}
:
intuitively, if
\lambda
is too large, then there is too much regularization, and if
\lambda
is too small, then there is too little.

Assumption 3

(Regularization Parameter)
.

There exist universal constants
C_{4}
and
C_{5}
(possibly depending on
\kappa
) such that
C_{4}\sqrt{d}\leq\lambda/\sigma\leq C_{5}\sqrt{d}
.

The choice above matches the order of the regularization parameter taken in
Candès and Plan 2011
to yield consistent estimation, and hence this choice of
\lambda
is order-wise optimal. Throughout we assume that
\lambda
is fixed
a priori
.

Main Results

We are now prepared to state our main result,
Theorem

1
, which relates the the properties of
\bm{U}^{(0)}
and
\bm{Z}^{(\lambda)}
to
\bm{Z}_{{\sf HT}}
and
\bm{Z}_{{\sf ST}}^{(\lambda)}
respectively.

Theorem 1
.

Suppose that
1
,
2
and
3
hold.
There exists a constant
C>0
depending on our assumptions such that for all
n
sufficiently large,
for any
\varepsilon>C\exp(-cdr)
, and for any
1-
Lipschitz function
\phi:\mathbb{R}^{d\times d}\to\mathbb{R}
,

\displaystyle\begin{split}\mathbb{P}\bigg\{&\bigg|\phi\bigg(\frac{1}{\sqrt{dr}}\bm{U}^{(0)}\bm{U}^{(0)\top}\bigg)-\mathbb{E}_{\bm{H}}\phi\big(\frac{1}{\sqrt{dr}}\bm{Z}_{{\sf HT}}\big)\bigg|>\varepsilon+C\sigma\sqrt{\frac{r}{\gamma_{n}}}\bigg\}\\
&\leq O\bigg(\exp(-cd)+\exp(-cdr)+\exp(-cn)+\frac{1}{\varepsilon^{2}}\exp(-cdr\varepsilon^{4})+\exp(-\frac{(dr)^{2}}{n}\varepsilon^{4})\bigg);\end{split}

(11)

\displaystyle\begin{split}\mathbb{P}\bigg\{&\bigg|\phi\bigg(\frac{1}{\sqrt{dr}}\bm{Z}^{(\lambda)}\bigg)-\mathbb{E}_{\bm{H}}\phi\big(\frac{1}{\sqrt{dr}}\bm{Z}_{{\sf ST}}^{(\lambda)}\big)\bigg|>\varepsilon+C\sigma\frac{\sqrt{r}}{\gamma_{n}}\bigg\}\\
&\leq O\bigg(\exp(-cd)+\exp(-cdr)+\exp(-cn)+\frac{1}{\varepsilon^{2}}\exp(-cdr\varepsilon^{4})+\exp(-\frac{(dr)^{2}}{n}\varepsilon^{4})\bigg)\end{split}

(12)

The same result continues to hold if
\bm{Z}^{(\lambda)}
is replaced with
\bm{U}^{(\lambda)}\bm{U}^{(\lambda)\top}
.

We note that the scaling by
\frac{1}{\sqrt{dr}}
is simply the scaling required in order for the error to be
\Theta(1)
. In addition, as we will demonstrate in the proof (see
Theorem

4
), the error term
C\sqrt{r}/\gamma_{n}
for the convex estimator in the second line can be eliminated by replacing
\bm{Z}^{(\lambda)}_{{\sf ST}}
with a slightly different quantity (see
Section

3
for details). Furthermore, the requirement that
\varepsilon>C\exp(-cdr)
is only technical, since if
\varepsilon\ll\exp(-cdr)
, the probability bound becomes essentially vacuous (as it becomes arbitrarily close to one). Finally, we note that the one of the probability bounds also requires that
n\ll(dr)^{2}
. However, as throughout this paper we are primarily interested in
d^{2}\gg n
, so this requirement is not stringent.

Before discussing the implications of
Theorem

1
, we first state the following corollary demonstrating its utility. The proof can be found in
Appendix

E
.

Corollary 1
.

Consider the asymptotic regime where
d\to\infty
with
r
held fixed and
n=n(d)
satisfies
d\ll n(d)\ll d^{2}
. Let
\{\bm{M}(d)\}_{d=1}^{\infty}
denote a sequence of rank
r
signal matrices, and suppose the eigenvalues of
\bm{M}(d)/\sqrt{d}
converge deterministically to some limits
\lambda_{1},\dots,\lambda_{r}
satisfying
1
. Suppose further that
1
,
2
and
3
are satisfied for all
n
and
d
, and suppose
\sigma
is fixed. Let
\bm{Z}^{(\lambda)}=\bm{Z}^{(\lambda)}(d)
denote the sequence of convex minimizers, and let
\bm{U}^{(0)}=\bm{U}^{(0)}(d)
be defined similarly.
Then it holds that

\displaystyle\liminf_{d\to\infty}\frac{\|\bm{Z}^{(\lambda)}-\bm{M}(d)\|_{F}^{2}}{\|\bm{U}^{(0)}\bm{U}^{(0)\top}-\bm{M}(d)\|_{F}^{2}}\geq 1,

(13)

where the limit is in probability, the infimum is over the sequence of problem instances, and strict inequality can hold for particular sequences of matrices
\{\bm{M}(d)\}_{d=1}^{\infty}
.

Note that the quantity
\|\bm{U}^{(0)}\bm{U}^{(0)\top}-\bm{M}\|_{F}^{2}
is (a rescaled version of) the excess risk for the estimator
\bm{U}^{(0)}\bm{U}^{(0)\top}
, with a similar interpretation for
\|\bm{Z}^{(\lambda)}-\bm{M}\|_{F}^{2}
.
Furthermore, (
13
) implies that asymptotically
\|\bm{Z}^{(\lambda)}-\bm{M}\|_{F}\geq\|\bm{U}^{(0)}\bm{U}^{(0)\top}-\bm{M}\|_{F}
, with strict inequality possible. In particular, this implies that the convex estimator has a test error larger than the nonconvex estimator, and hence is inadmissible with respect to the squared loss.

Figure 1:
For varying
n\in\{1000,2000,3000\}
, we generate
\bm{M}
by first drawing a random matrix
\bm{U}
of dimension
n\times r
with entries
\mathcal{N}(0,1)
and setting
\bm{\widetilde{M}}:=\bm{UU}^{\top}
. We then define
\bm{M}
by setting the smallest nonzero singular value of
\bm{\widetilde{M}}
to be
\sqrt{d}
. We control the SNR by varying
\sigma\in\{1/2,1,2\}
corresponding to an SNR of
\{2,1,1/2\}
respectively. We take
\lambda=0.5\sigma\sqrt{d}
in all simulations. To estimate
\bm{U}^{(0)}
we run gradient descent on
f^{(0)}_{{\sf ncvx}}
starting from the ground truth with step size
2\times 10^{-4}
for
d=50
and
10^{-4}
for
d=75
respectively, and we stop when the gradient is below
.01
. Similarly, we estimate
\bm{Z}^{(\lambda)}
by proximal gradient descent with step size
.01
, and we stopped when the gradient was below
.01
. Our random seed was set to be
492025
. The y-axis represents the error
\frac{1}{2d}\|\bm{\widehat{M}}-\bm{M}\|_{F}^{2}
, with
\bm{\widehat{M}}
either equal to
\bm{Z}^{(\lambda)}
or
\bm{U}^{(0)}\bm{U}^{(0)\top}
. The solid line represents our empirical estimate and the points correspond to our theory, with the empirical estimate averaged over five iterations.

Fig.

1
displays the behavior illustrated by
Corollary

1
for varying levels of the signal-to-noise ratio (left to right) and varying
d
(top to bottom).
Corollary

1
shows that the nonconvex estimator dominates the convex estimator in least squares error, so we plot the error
\frac{1}{2d}\|\bm{\widehat{M}}-\bm{M}\|_{F}^{2}
under our model, with
\bm{\widehat{M}}
either
\bm{U}^{(0)}\bm{U}^{(0)\top}
(blue) or
\bm{Z}^{(\lambda)}
(red). The dotted lines represent the theoretical prediction and the solid lines represent our empirical prediction, and the figure corroborates our theory for high SNR, the nonconvex estimator outperforms the convex estimator, and the approximation becomes more accurate as
n
and
d
increase. Furthermore, when the SNR is less than one (something not covered by our theory), we find that the convex estimator outperforms the nonconvex estimator (the last panel). This mirrors matrix denoising as discussed in
Gavish and Donoho 2014
. Interestingly, despite lack of theoretical backing, the error of the nonconvex estimator (the solid blue line) still seems to approximate the error of matrix hard thresholding (the dotted blue line) as in the case of high SNR. It would be interesting to derive an analogue of
Theorem

1
in such a “weak signal” regime.

We now highlight several features of
Theorem

1
:

•

Interpretation
. The main benefit of
Theorem

1
is to translate the behavior of the (complicated) estimators
\bm{U}^{(0)}
and
\bm{Z}^{(\lambda)}
into the behavior of the (simpler) estimators
\bm{Z}_{{\sf HT}}
and
\bm{Z}_{{\sf ST}}^{(\lambda)}
. Specifically,
Theorem

1
shows that Lipschitz functions of
\bm{U}^{(0)}\bm{U}^{(0)\top}
(or
\bm{Z}^{(\lambda)}
) concentrate around expected values of Lipschitz functions of
\bm{Z}_{{\sf HT}}
(or
\bm{Z}_{{\sf ST}}^{(\lambda)}
),
with the error decaying roughly of order
o(1)
when
\frac{n}{dr^{2}}\to\infty
. We elect to state
Theorem

1
in terms of a Lipschitz function
\phi:\mathbb{R}^{d\times d}\to\mathbb{R}
to allow for generality. Furthermore, convergence of Lipschitz functions is equivalent to convergence in distribution, so one (informal) interpretation of
Theorem

1
could be that
\bm{U}^{(0)}\bm{U}^{(0)\top}
(resp.
\bm{Z}^{(\lambda)}
) is approximately equal in distribution to
\bm{Z}_{{\sf HT}}
(resp.
\bm{Z}_{{\sf ST}}^{(\lambda)}
). Also, throughout our analysis of
\bm{U}^{(0)}
we assume that the rank
r
is known. Therefore, another (informal) interpretation of
Corollary

1
is that knowledge of the rank
r
improves estimation. It is unclear whether the estimator
\bm{U}^{(0)}
with
r
over-estimated will continue to dominate
\bm{Z}^{(\lambda)}
, which is a regime of interest as
r
is typically unknown in practice. However, such a result seems out of reach with our techniques, so we leave such analyses to future work.

•

High-Dimensional Asymptotics of Both Estimators
. In the asymptotic regime
n\gg dr^{2}
, one can use
Theorem

1
to derive the leading-order constant for the asymptotic test error as in
Corollary

1
, or it can be used for statistical inference. For example, suppose one is interested in providing confidence intervals for entries of
\bm{M}
. Our results imply that the nonconvex estimator satisfies

\displaystyle\bigg(\bm{U}^{(0)}\bm{U}^{(0)\top}\bigg)_{ij}\approx\mathcal{P}_{r}\bigg(\bm{M}+\sigma\bm{H}\bigg)_{ij},

where the “
\approx
” holds in the sense of distribution. One can therefore derive asymptotically valid confidence intervals using techniques for matrix denoising as in
Chen et al. 2021a
. Furthermore, our analysis shows that the convex estimator requires debiasing for statistical inference, whereas the nonconvex estimator is
already approximately unbiased!

•

Optimality of Assumptions
.
When
d^{2}\gg n\gg d
and
r\asymp 1
, by taking
\varepsilon\asymp d^{-1/4}\log(n)
,
Theorem

1
shows concentration at the rate
\widetilde{O}(d^{-1/4}+\sqrt{\frac{d}{n}})
for the nonconvex estimator and concentration at the rate
O(d^{-1/4}+\frac{d}{n})
for the convex estimator. The quantity
d^{-1/4}
probably has an incorrect fractional power on
d
(it should be the parametric rate
d^{-1/2}
), although this is a known deficiency of CGMT-style analyses, which often result in a suboptimal dependence on dimension when deriving concentration bounds
(
Chandrasekher et al. 2023
;
Celentano et al. 2023
;
Miolane and Montanari 2021
)
. The additional error terms depending on the ratio
\frac{d}{n}
are likely necessary without significantly modifying the results, since consistency is only guaranteed in this regime when
n\gg d
. It would be of interest to improve the dependence on these rates.

Our result mimics asymptotics for sparse regression with
s
nonzero coordinates and
p
dimensions in the regime
\frac{s\log(p/s)}{n}\to 0

(
Javanmard and Montanari 2018
;
Javanmard and Montanari 2014b
;
Bellec and Zhang 2023
)
.
While there already exists a rich literature on sparse high-dimensional linear regression, similar analyses for matrix sensing are still in their infancy. To the best of our knowledge,
Theorem

1
is the first result of its kind for both the convex and nonconvex estimators in this high-dimensional setup.

The choice of the order of the tuning parameter
\lambda
is order-wise optimal (though implicitly requires knowledge of the noise level). In practice one may wish to further optimize over
\lambda
, but our results may not cover this approach, as such an analysis would require a
uniform
control with respect to
\lambda
, and our results only apply for a (fixed) choice of
\lambda
. It is of interest to determine whether there are settings for which optimizing over
\lambda
can yield better error rates, but we leave such analyses to future work.

Proof Strategy and Novelty

We now highlight the key steps and novelty of our proof.
Roughly speaking, given a Lipschitz function
\phi
, the major intuition behind our proof can be intuitively understood via the following “approximate equivalences:”

\displaystyle\phi\big(\bm{Z}^{(\lambda)}\big)

\displaystyle\approx\phi\big(\bm{U}^{(\lambda)}\bm{U}^{(\lambda)\top}\big)\approx\phi\big(\bm{Z}_{{\sf ST}}^{(\lambda)}\big);

(14)

\displaystyle\phi\big(\bm{U}^{(0)}\bm{U}^{(0)\top}\big)

\displaystyle\approx\phi\big(\bm{U}^{(\lambda)}_{{\sf deb}}\bm{U}^{(\lambda)\top}_{{\sf deb}}\big)\approx\phi\big(\bm{Z}^{(\lambda)}_{{\sf deb}}\big)\approx\phi\big(\bm{Z}_{{\sf HT}}\big),

(15)

where
\bm{U}^{(\lambda)}_{{\sf deb}}
and
\bm{Z}_{{\sf deb}}^{(\lambda)}
are “debiased” estimators defined via

\displaystyle\bm{Z}_{{\sf deb}}^{(\lambda)}

\displaystyle:=\mathcal{P}_{{\mathrm{rank}-r}}\bigg(\bm{Z}^{(\lambda)}+\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\bigg(y_{i}-\langle\bm{X}_{i},\bm{Z}^{(\lambda)}\rangle\bigg)\bm{X}_{i}\bigg);

(16)

\displaystyle\bm{U}_{{\sf deb}}^{(\lambda)}

\displaystyle:=\bm{U}^{(\lambda)}\bigg(\bm{I}+\lambda\big(\bm{U}^{(\lambda)\top}\bm{U}^{(\lambda)}\big)^{-1}\bigg)^{1/2}.

(17)

We will elaborate on each of these approximate equivalences, as well as further discussion of
\bm{U}_{{\sf deb}}^{(\lambda)}
and
\bm{Z}_{{\sf deb}}^{(\lambda)}
in the subsequent paragraphs. For convenience at we have also included a diagram of the main steps of the proof in
Fig.

2
.

\bm{U}^{(\lambda)}\bm{U}^{(\lambda)\top}

=\bm{Z}^{(\lambda)}

(
Theorem

2
)

\bm{U}_{{\sf deb}}^{(\lambda)}\bm{U}_{{\sf deb}}^{(\lambda)\top}

=\bm{Z}^{(\lambda)}_{{\sf deb}}

(
Theorem

5
)

\phi\big(\bm{Z}^{(\lambda)}\big)

\approx\mathbb{E}\phi\big(\bm{Z}^{(\lambda)}_{{\sf ST}}\big)

(
Theorem

4
)

(

)

\phi\big(\bm{Z}^{(\lambda)}_{{\sf deb}}\big)

\approx\phi\big(\bm{Z}_{{\sf HT}}\big)

(
Theorem

6
)

(
Lemma

8
)

\bm{U}^{(0)}\bm{U}^{(0)\top}

\approx\bm{U}_{{\sf deb}}^{(\lambda)}\bm{U}_{{\sf deb}}^{(\lambda)\top}

(
Theorem

7
)

\phi(\bm{U}^{(0)}\bm{U}^{(0)\top}\big)

\approx\mathbb{E}\phi\big(\bm{Z}_{{\sf HT}}\big)

(
Theorem

)

(

)

Figure 2:
Diagram of Proof Dependencies

Theorem

2
: Equivalence of Convex and (Regularized) Nonconvex Estimators,
\bm{Z}^{(\lambda)}=\bm{U}^{(\lambda)}\bm{U}^{(\lambda)\top}
, via Study of Local Minima.

The first equivalence we establish concerns the minima of
f^{(\lambda)}_{{\sf ncvx}}
and
g^{(\lambda)}_{{\sf cvx}}
with the same regularization parameter. Indeed; recall from the variational form of the nuclear norm that for all positive semidefinite matrices
\bm{Z}\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times d}
of rank at most
r
it holds that

\displaystyle\|\bm{Z}\|_{*}=\min_{\bm{U}\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times r}:\bm{UU}^{\top}=\bm{Z}}\|\bm{U}\|_{F}^{2}.

(18)

Consequently, since
f^{(\lambda)}_{{\sf ncvx}}
includes a penalty term of
\|\bm{U}\|_{F}^{2}
and
g^{(\lambda)}_{{\sf cvx}}
includes a penalty term of
\|\bm{Z}\|_{*}
it is natural that perhaps the local minima of these objectives ought to “track” each other.
Since
\bm{U}^{(\lambda)}
is computed with a
pre-specified
rank
r
, to ensure that local minima of
f^{(\lambda)}_{{\sf ncvx}}
and
g^{(\lambda)}_{{\sf cvx}}
correspond, one needs to guarantee that the minimizer of
g^{(\lambda)}_{{\sf cvx}}
is rank at most
r
.

It turns out that there are simple conditions on the noise
\{\varepsilon_{i}\}_{i=1}^{n}
and the operator
\mathcal{X}
such that the minimizer of
g_{{\sf cvx}}^{(\lambda)}(\bm{Z})
is rank at most
r
. First, a simple condition is that the noise
\{\varepsilon_{i}\}_{i=1}^{n}
is sufficiently well-behaved, which can be handled directly via techniques from random matrix theory. In addition, the other condition is that
\mathcal{X}^{*}\mathcal{X}
behaves approximately as the identity operator on rank at most
2r
matrices, which is known as the
restricted isometry property
in the literature. In particular, one formulation of the restricted isometry property is that
\mathcal{X}
satisfies

\displaystyle(1-\delta_{2r})\|\bm{A}\|_{F}\leq\|\mathcal{X}^{*}\mathcal{X}(\bm{A})\|\leq(1+\delta_{2r})\|\bm{A}\|_{F},

(19)

for all matrices
\bm{A}
of rank at most
2r
, where
\|\cdot\|
is the operator norm. Here,
\delta_{2r}\geq 0
is the
restricted isometry constant
(roughly speaking, a quantitative measurement of the condition number of
\mathcal{X}^{*}\mathcal{X}
on rank
2r
matrices). We encapsulate these “good” properties in the “good” event
\mathcal{E}_{{\sf Good}}
defined formally in (
20
).

In order to formally prove
Theorem

2
, we first establish that first-order critical points of
f^{(\lambda)}_{{\sf ncvx}}
and
g^{(\lambda)}_{{\sf cvx}}
are in correspondence provided they lie within radius
R=O(\sigma\sqrt{dr})
of
\bm{M}
, which allows us to eliminate potentially bad critical points. Next, we show that any local minimum of
f^{(\lambda)}_{{\sf ncvx}}
satisfying the second-order necessary conditions for a local minimum must lie within such a region of
\bm{M}
on the event
\mathcal{E}_{{\sf Good}}
, which in turn yields the equivalence of the minima. As a byproduct of this proof, on this same event,
\bm{Z}^{(\lambda)}
is rank at most
r
and is within radius
R
of
\bm{M}
, which turns out to be a useful fact for the subsequent analysis.

Our arguments here bear some similarity to a number of similar lines of work in matrix completion establishing connections between convex and nonconvex procedures
(
Yang and Ma 2023
;
Chen et al. 2021b
;
Chen et al. 2021c
;
Wang et al. 2025
)
, most notably
Chen et al. 2020
. However, unlike
Chen et al. 2020
, we prove the equivalence by directly studying the second-order necessary conditions for a local minimum, whereas
Chen et al. 2020
provide an inductive argument by considering the iterates of gradient descent initialized at the (unknown) ground truth
\bm{M}
. By starting directly from the second-order necessary conditions we bypass the inductive arguments, and our results are therefore algorithm-agnostic.

Theorem

4
: Concentration of
\phi\big(\bm{Z}^{(\lambda)}\big)
about
\mathbb{E}\phi\big(\bm{Z}_{{\sf ST}}^{(\lambda)}\big)
via Matrix CGMT.

Having shown the (exact) equivalence of convex and (regularized) nonconvex estimators, our next major technical argument is to demonstrate the approximate equivalence of minima of
g^{(\lambda)}_{{\sf cvx}}
with
\bm{Z}_{{\sf ST}}^{(\lambda)}
, the soft-thresholding estimator. This result is significantly different in spirit from the previous argument concerning local minima, as the form of approximate equivalence herein is taken to mean that Lipschitz functions of the former concentrate about expected values of Lipschitz functions of the latter as in
Theorem

1
.

In
Theorem

4
we demonstrate that
\bm{Z}^{(\lambda)}
approximates another soft-thresholding estimator which we then show approximates
\bm{Z}^{(\lambda)}_{{\sf ST}}
asymptotically via
Lemma

7
.
It is worth emphasizing that
Theorem

4
is able to yield precise asymptotics in the regime
\frac{dr^{2}}{n}\asymp C
as long as
1
,
2
and
3
hold. To prove this approximate equivalence, we leverage Gaussian comparison inequalities via a novel generalization of the Convex Gaussian Min-Max Theorem of
Thrampoulidis et al. 2015a
tailored to matrix ensembles. We dub this result, which may be of independent interest (see
Theorem

3
), the “Matrix CGMT.”

Informally, the (vector) CGMT allows one to translate probabilistic statements about the optimal value of one min-max problem over Gaussian processes into the optimal value of another, often much simpler, min-max problem with different Gaussian processes.
This technique has been used to great success in a number of works on high-dimensional regression, though we leave a more in-depth literature review to
Section

1.5
. Our work is most related to the analysis in
Celentano et al. 2023
.

Many analyses based on the CGMT follow a similar recipe:

1.

First, show that the empirical minimization problem (e.g.
\min g^{(\lambda)}_{{\sf cvx}}
) can be written as an appropriate convex-concave min-max problem, and apply the CGMT to translate probabilistic statements about this min-max problem into the “simpler” min-max problem (which we refer to as the “CGMT objective”).

2.

Next, study the behavior of the CGMT objective about its optimal value to derive probabilistic statements about minima of the empirical minimization problem.

Step one above is a straightforward generalization of the arguments of
Celentano et al. 2023
to the matrix setting via the Matrix CGMT. After carrying out this argument, we arrive at the “MCGMT objective”
L_{\lambda}^{(n)}(\bm{Z})
defined in (
311
).

The second step (studying the probabilistic behavior of the CGMT objective) is more complicated. Unlike
Celentano et al. 2023
, the function
L_{\lambda}^{(n)}(\cdot)
is not necessarily strongly convex on
d\times d
matrices in a neighborhood of
\bm{Z}_{{\sf ST}}^{(\lambda)}
, and analyzing its probabilistic behavior becomes more difficult due to this lack of strong convexity. However, by our previous analysis, we are able to restrict
L_{\lambda}^{(n)}
to rank at most
r
matrices within a certain radius of
\bm{M}
, and one of our key insights is to demonstrate that this restriction results in a form of strong convexity. This restricted strong convexity mimics similar types of restricted strong convexity in other low-rank matrix optimization (e.g.,
Chi et al. 2019
). However, to the best of our knowledge, we are the first to combine these arguments with the CGMT, and our analysis requires a number of additional technical considerations beyond those required for the LASSO in
Celentano et al. 2023
.

Theorems

5
and
6
: Concentration of
\phi(\bm{U}^{(\lambda)}_{{\sf deb}}\bm{U}^{(\lambda)\top}_{{\sf deb}})
and
\phi(\bm{Z}^{(\lambda)}_{{\sf deb}})
about
\mathbb{E}\phi(\bm{Z}_{{\sf ST}})
.

Recall that our analysis uses two intermediate quantities
\bm{U}^{(\lambda)}_{{\sf deb}}
and
\bm{Z}_{{\sf deb}}^{(\lambda)}

that can be computed from
\bm{U}^{(\lambda)}
and
\bm{Z}^{(\lambda)}
respectively.
While we view these two quantities as theoretical tools, in principle
\bm{Z}_{{\sf deb}}^{(\lambda)}
and
\bm{U}_{{\sf deb}}^{(\lambda)}
could be used for statistical inference as they are computable from data. We show in
Theorem

5
that
\bm{Z}_{{\sf deb}}^{(\lambda)}=\bm{U}^{(\lambda)}_{{\sf deb}}\bm{U}^{(\lambda)\top}_{{\sf deb}}
with high probability.

To understand the reasoning behind introducing these estimators, consider the matrix denoising problem momentarily. In this case, the quantity
\bm{Z}_{{\sf ST}}^{(\lambda)}
has its leading
r
singular values shrunken by
\lambda
, which leads to a bias. In order to debias this estimator, one would ideally re-impute all of the singular values of
\bm{Z}_{{\sf ST}}^{(\lambda)}
. However, it turns out that only imputing the leading few singular values is sufficient, as keeping the remaining singular values to zero serves to act as additional denoising. Therefore, the “debiasing” operation one arrives at is simply to add back
\lambda
to the leading
r
singular values, which results in
\bm{Z}_{{\sf HT}}
.

Observe that operation
\bm{U}^{(\lambda)}\bm{U}^{(\lambda)\top}\mapsto\bm{U}_{{\sf deb}}^{(\lambda)}\bm{U}_{{\sf deb}}^{(\lambda)\top}
can be equivalently be expressed as adding
\lambda
to the leading
r
singular values of
\bm{Z}^{(\lambda)}
. Consequently, both the operations
\bm{U}^{(\lambda)}\bm{U}^{(\lambda)\top}\mapsto\bm{U}^{(\lambda)}_{{\sf deb}}\bm{U}^{(\lambda)\top}_{{\sf deb}}
and
\bm{Z}_{{\sf ST}}^{(\lambda)}\mapsto\bm{Z}_{{\sf HT}}
can be understood as adding
\lambda
to the nonzero singular values. Since we have already shown that
\phi\big(\bm{Z}^{(\lambda)}\big)
concentrates about
\mathbb{E}\phi\big(\bm{Z}^{(\lambda)}_{{\sf ST}}\big)
, this discussion shows that it is intuitive to argue that
\phi\big(\bm{Z}_{{\sf deb}}^{(\lambda)}\big)
concentrates about
\mathbb{E}\phi\big(\bm{Z}_{{\sf HT}}\big)
. This result is accomplished in
Theorem

6
, which shows that
\phi(\bm{Z}_{{\sf deb}}^{(\lambda)})
concentrates around around another hard thresholding estimator, which we then show approximates
\bm{Z}_{{\sf HT}}
asymptotically via
Lemma

8
. The proof of
Theorem

6
follows by applying
Theorem

4
, where we use the fact that the debiasing operator (i.e., adding
\lambda
to the nonzero singular values) is a Lipschitz function on an appropriate domain.

Theorem

7
: Concentration of
\bm{U}_{{\sf deb}}^{(\lambda)}
about
\bm{U}^{(0)}
.

Our final major technical contribution is to demonstrate that
\bm{U}_{{\sf deb}}^{(\lambda)}
and
\bm{U}^{(0)}
are close in Frobenius norm (up to some nonidentifiable orthogonal transformation). Informally, our main idea is to show that
\bm{U}_{{\sf deb}}^{(\lambda)}
is an approximate local minimum for
f^{(0)}_{{\sf ncvx}}
, hence “closeness” follows via a Taylor expansion argument. However, as we demonstrate in
Theorem

7
, of all of the steps of our proofs, this step is the only step that essentially requires that
n\gg dr^{2}
. In order to formally prove this result, we first show that
\nabla f^{(0)}_{{\sf ncvx}}\big(\bm{U}_{{\sf deb}}^{(\lambda)}\big)
, the gradient of the unregularized loss function
f^{(0)}_{{\sf ncvx}}
evaluated at
\bm{U}_{{\sf deb}}^{(\lambda)}
,
is close to zero with high probability. In addition, since
\bm{U}^{(0)}
is a local minimum of
f^{(0)}_{{\sf ncvx}}
we show that on the event
\mathcal{E}_{{\sf Good}}
the second derivative of
f^{(0)}_{{\sf ncvx}}
is sufficiently well-behaved in a neighborhood of
\bm{U}^{(0)}
, and
\bm{U}^{(\lambda)}_{{\sf deb}}
falls in this neighborhood on this event.

Related Work

In this section we review related work. First, we consider low-rank matrix optimization both in the noiseless and noisy settings. Next our main technical tools are closely related to similar tools used in the literature on precise asymptotics for high-dimensional linear regression, and we review these related works as well.

Low-Rank Matrix Optimization
. A number of works have studied factorized nonconvex approaches to low-rank matrix estimation, with several works focusing on the optimization landscape and geometry
(
Ge et al. 2017
;
Bhojanapalli et al. 2016
)
.
There has also been a few works considering the geometry with
r
overparameterized
(
Zhang 2021
;
Ma et al. 2023
;
Ma and Fattahi 2023
)
. Subsequent research has also been devoted to studying the iterates of gradient descent and its variants, including settings where the parameter
r
is overspecified and the initialization is random

(
Stöger and Soltanolkotabi 2021
;
Soltanolkotabi et al. 2025
;
Ma et al. 2023
)
or the matrix
\bm{M}
is ill-conditioned
(
Tong et al. 2021
;
Zhang et al. 2023
;
Xu et al. 2023
)
. For a more complete list of references, see the survey
Chi et al. 2019
.

For matrix sensing the primary focus of this line of work has been the noiseless setting, and one theme that has emerged is that when the rank is correctly specified, all local minima are in fact global minima, and all other critical points are “strict saddles,” meaning there is a search direction along which the Hessian is negative. In particular, in the noiseless setting, all local minima can be shown to yield the ground truth signal matrix. Therefore,
our results can be viewed as an extension from the noiseless setting to the noisy setting: in the noisy setting all local minima behave asymptotically equivalently to the truncated SVD of the corresponding matrix denoising problem, which matches the ground truth in the noiseless case. A major novelty of our work is in demonstrating that this form of “asymptotic equivalence” holds in a precise distributional sense (in the sense of Lipschitz functions).

Our work is also closely related to several works on low-rank matrix estimation under various statistical models. For example,
Chen et al. 2021c
study noisy blind deconvolution,
Candès et al. 2015
;
Chen et al. 2019a
;
Cai et al. 2016a
study phase retrieval,
Chen et al. 2021b
study robust PCA,
Chen et al. 2020
study noisy matrix completion, and
Ma et al. 2020
;
Charisopoulos et al. 2021
consider several different low-rank problems. From a technical point of view, our analysis differs significantly from these works, who rely on a “leave-one-out” argument to study the iterates of nonconvex procedures. Typically the focus of these works has been to study the statistical error
rates
, whereas our focus is on the precise asymptotic statistical behavior. A work markedly similar to ours is
Chen et al. 2019b
, in which the authors consider developing confidence regions for the convex estimator in matrix completion via a debiasing procedure. Besides the fact that they consider matrix completion, whereas we consider matrix sensing, unlike their work our results apply to both convex and nonconvex estimators directly, whereas the results of
Chen et al. 2019b
only apply to the convex estimator, though they also consider an analogue of
\bm{U}^{(\lambda)}
. The focus of our work is on explicitly studying the statistical behavior of
\bm{U}^{(0)}
and
\bm{Z}^{(\lambda)}
, whereas the focus of their work is on statistical inference.

Perhaps the closest related works to our setting are the works
Chandrasekher et al. 2024
;
Chandrasekher et al. 2023
who derive precise predictions for iterative procedures in high-dimensional regimes similar to the one we consider herein. In
Chandrasekher et al. 2024
the authors study alternating minimization for rank one matrix sensing, though the statistical model is significantly different from the one we consider herein. In addition,
Chandrasekher et al. 2023
uses the CGMT to derive iterate-by-iterate predictions for nonconvex procedures for variants of high-dimensional regression, provided that each iteration minimizes some convex problem.
However, both
Chandrasekher et al. 2024
and
Chandrasekher et al. 2023
rely heavily on the assumption of
sample-splitting
, meaning that a fresh set of data is used at each iteration of their algorithm. In contrast, our work has no such assumption, as our results apply directly to local minima regardless of algorithm.

Precise Asymptotics for High-Dimensional Statistical Models
. Besides the work
Chandrasekher et al. 2023
, the CGMT has been used in a number of works studying the asymptotics of estimators of various procedures in high dimensions.
For example,
Thrampoulidis et al. 2015b
;
Thrampoulidis et al. 2018
studied regularized linear regression and M-estimation respectively,
Javanmard et al. 2020
;
Javanmard and Soltanolkotabi 2022
considered adversarial training,
Liang and Sur 2022
;
Donhauser et al. 2022
;
Wang et al. 2022
;
Stojanovic et al. 2024
;
Han and Xu 2023
considered minimum norm interpolators for regression and classification,
Montanari et al. 2025
considered margin-based classfication, and
Taheri et al. 2020
;
Taheri et al. 2021
;
Han 2023
;
Loureiro et al. 2021
;
Salehi et al. 2019
considered other variants of linear models. Our model is significantly different from these works, as our model is based on matrix-valued observations, necessitating a generalization of the CGMT used in these prior works to matrix ensembles (
Theorem

3
).

As mentioned previously, our analyses are partially motivated by those in
Celentano et al. 2023
, who provide precise statistical guarantees for the LASSO and its debiased counterpart under general Gaussian measurements in the
inconsistency regime
(i.e., the asymptotic regime where minimax error does not vanish asymptotically). While some of our techniques are similar, our focus is significantly different, as we primarily focus on a regime in which minimax error
does
vanish asymptotically, though we only require this assumption in the final step of our proof.
However, we also study the minimizer of the nonconvex objective function
f_{{\sf ncvx}}^{(\lambda)}
, to which there is no such immediate analogue in sparse linear regression. In essence, this difference is due to the fact that singular values are
ordered
automatically by magnitude, whereas linear regression coefficients do not have any such ordering
a priori
.

Finally, the debiased estimators we consider in this work are motivated by
Chen et al. 2019b
, though similar forms of
\bm{Z}_{{\sf deb}}^{(\lambda)}
have appeared elsewhere
(
Xia 2019
;
Cai et al. 2016b
)
. The estimator
\bm{Z}_{{\sf deb}}^{(\lambda)}
without the rank
r
projection step is also similar to the debiased LASSO first introduced in
(
Zhang and Zhang 2014
)
and studied further in the works of
Javanmard and Montanari 2014a
;
Javanmard and Montanari 2018
;
Miolane and Montanari 2021
;
Celentano et al. 2023
, among others.

Discussion

In this paper we have established the precise asymptotic behavior of both the convex and nonconvex formulations to the matrix sensing problem. We have seen that even by picking the parameter
\lambda
to be order-wise optimal
a priori
, the nonconvex estimator performs better in terms of its generalization ability. The heart of this result relies on an intimate connection between the nonconvex minima and the debiased convex minima, which we show to be asymptotically “equivalent” in the regime
\gamma_{n}/r\to\infty
.

While we believe our analysis to shed new light on the matrix sensing problem, a number of open problems remain, which we detail below.

•

Asymmetric matrices:
In our analysis we have assumed throughout that the underlying matrices (both the observations and the matrix to be estimated) are symmetric. This assumption greatly simplifies our analyses, but in practice matrices need not be symmetric. The paper of
Ma et al. 2021
showed that in the noiseless setting gradient descent on asymmetric matrices without additional regularization converges to a local minimum, but extending this result to the noisy setting would require significant additional considerations for both the left and right factors.

•

Choice of
r
:
We assumed throughout that
r
was known
a priori
. Such an assumption is unrealistic in practice, as
r
is often overspecified. There has been some work studying such a regime
(
Stöger and Soltanolkotabi 2021
;
Soltanolkotabi et al. 2025
;
Ma et al. 2023
)
, but these works do not obtain any statistical characterizations similar to the one found herein.

•

The inconsistency regime:
Our work assumed that
\gamma_{n}/r\to\infty
(or at least
\gamma_{n}\geq C_{3}r
), which is essentially the minimal assumption in order to have consistency up to the factor of
r
. Extension of our results to the inconsistency regime will likely require new arguments, as our form of “approximate equivalence” of
\bm{U}_{{\sf deb}}^{(\lambda)}
and
\bm{U}^{(0)}
relied heavily on this regime.

•

Heavy-tailed noise:
Our analysis requires that
\bm{\varepsilon}
is a Gaussian random vector, though the extension to subgaussian noise is likely minor. However, extending our results to encompass the heavy-tailed setting may require analyzing a new estimator that incorporates a robust loss function.

•

Weak signals:
In
1
we require that
\lambda_{r}/\sigma\geq C_{1}\sqrt{r}
, which implies that
\bm{M}
has enough signal. In our numerical experiments we have seen that the nonconvex estimator no longer dominates the convex estimator in weak signal regimes, a regime not covered by our theory. It would be of interest to pin down all regimes where the nonconvex estimator dominates the convex estimator (or vice versa).

•

Matrix sensing as a testbed for neural networks:
Finally, one of our major results shows that the nonconvex formulation with correctly specified rank dominates the nonconvex formulation. Insofar as matrix sensing can be viewed as a toy model for two-layer neural networks, it may be interesting to study how to extend our results to deep neural networks with many hidden layers.

Paper Organization

The rest of this paper is organized as follows. First, in
Section

2
we demonstrate the equivalence of the convex and regularized nonconvex estimators, and in
Section

3
we study the precise high-dimensional behavior of the convex estimator.
Section

4
is dedicated to studying the debiased estimators, and
Section

5
is dedicated to the study of the concentration of the unregularized estimator about the debiased estimator. We finish with a proof of
Theorem

1
in
Section

6
. More detailed proofs are deferred to the appendices.

Equivalence of Convex and Regularized Nonconvex Estimators

In this section we demonstrate the equivalence of the convex and regularized nonconvex estimators; i.e,
Theorem

2
in
Fig.

2
. The result relies on the existence of a high-probability event
\mathcal{E}_{{\sf Good}}
such that
\bm{U}^{(\lambda)}\bm{U}^{(\lambda)\top}=\bm{Z}^{(\lambda)}
. To wit,
define the “good” event
\mathcal{E}_{{\sf Good}}
via

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}_{{\sf Good}}

\displaystyle:=\bigg\{\bigg\|\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i}\varepsilon_{i}\bm{X}_{i}\bigg\|\leq 8\sigma\sqrt{\frac{d}{n}}\bigg\}

(20a)

\displaystyle\bigcap\bigg\{(1-\delta_{2r})\|\bm{A}\|_{F}^{2}\leq\|\mathcal{X}(\bm{A})\|^{2}\leq(1+\delta_{2r})\|\bm{A}\|_{F}^{2}\text{ for all matrices $\bm{A}$ of rank at most $2r$}\bigg\}

(20b)

\displaystyle\bigcap\bigg\{\bigg\|\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i}\varepsilon_{i}\langle\bm{X}_{i},\bm{A}\rangle\bigg\|\leq C\sigma\sqrt{\frac{dr}{n}}\|\bm{A}\|_{F}\text{ for all matrices $\bm{A}$ of rank at most $2r$}\bigg\}.

(20c)

Here
\delta_{2r}>0
is a fixed constant. Intuitively, the first event (
20a
) ensures that the random matrix
\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i}\varepsilon_{i}\bm{X}_{i}
(which behaves as a random linear combination of
{\sf GOE}(d)
random matrices) does not deviate too far in spectral norm, which guarantees that the noise is sufficiently well-behaved. The next event (
20b
) is the well-known
restricted isometry property
for the operator
\mathcal{X}
. Many analyses for matrix sensing rely only on this assumption or something similar
(
Zhang 2021
;
Zheng and Lafferty 2015
;
Zhang et al. 2023
;
Ge et al. 2017
;
Ma et al. 2021
;
Soltanolkotabi et al. 2025
;
Stöger and Soltanolkotabi 2021
)
. The final event, (
20c
), argues that
\bm{X}_{i}
concentrates at a rate that holds
uniformly
over all matrices
\bm{A}
of rank at most
2r
. In this sense, this event blends both probabilistic concentration and something resembling the restricted isometry property (as it must hold uniformly over matrices of rank at most
2r
).

The following lemma shows that this event holds with high probability for an appropriate choice of
\delta_{2r}
.

Lemma 1
.

The event
\mathcal{E}_{{\sf Good}}
holds with probability at least
1-O(\exp(-cd))
whenever
n\geq Cdr/\delta_{2r}^{2}
. In particular,
\mathcal{E}_{{\sf Good}}
holds under
2
(i.e., we may take
\delta_{2r}=\frac{c}{\sqrt{r}}
).

Proof.

See
Section

A.1
.
∎

Next, the following theorem demonstrates that on the event
\mathcal{E}_{{\sf Good}}
, local minima of
f^{(\lambda)}_{{\sf ncvx}}
correspond to local (in fact, global) minima of
g^{(\lambda)}_{{\sf cvx}}
.

Theorem 2

(Relating Convex and Nonconvex Regularized Estimators)
.

Let
\bm{Z}^{(\lambda)}
denote (any) global minimum of
g^{(\lambda)}_{{\sf cvx}}
, and let
\bm{U}^{(\lambda)}
denote any critical point of
f^{(\lambda)}_{{\sf ncvx}}
satisfying the first and second-order necessary conditions for a local minimum. Then under
2
and
3
,
on the event
\mathcal{E}_{{\sf Good}}
defined in (
20
),

\displaystyle\bm{U}^{(\lambda)}\bm{U}^{(\lambda)\top}

\displaystyle=\bm{Z}^{(\lambda)}.

(21)

This result is entirely deterministic when
\mathcal{E}_{{\sf Good}}
holds.

Proof of
Theorem

2

In order to prove
Theorem

2
we first prove that the conclusion holds under a sufficient condition via the following lemma.

Lemma 2

(Relating the Convex and Nonconvex Regularized Estimators Under a Technical Condition)
.

Let
\bm{Z}^{(\lambda)}
denote the global minimum of
g^{(\lambda)}_{{\sf cvx}}
, and let
\bm{U}^{(\lambda)}
denote a stationary point of
f^{(\lambda)}_{{\sf ncvx}}
. Then as long as
\lambda\geq C_{4}\sigma\sqrt{d}
, and
\|\bm{U}^{(\lambda)}\bm{U}^{(\lambda)\top}-\bm{M}\|_{F}<\lambda/(2\delta_{2r})
, on the event
\mathcal{E}_{{\sf Good}}
it holds that

\displaystyle\bm{U}^{(\lambda)}\bm{U}^{(\lambda)\top}

\displaystyle=\bm{Z}^{(\lambda)}.

(22)

Proof of
Lemma

2
.

First, observe that any optimizer
\bm{Z}^{(\lambda)}
of
g^{(\lambda)}_{{\sf cvx}}
must satisfy the first-order necessary condition

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\bigg(\langle\bm{X}_{i},\bm{Z}^{(\lambda)}-\bm{M}\rangle/\sqrt{n}-\varepsilon_{i}\bigg)\bm{X}_{i}=-\lambda\bm{VV}^{\top}+\bm{W},

(23)

where
\bm{Z}^{(\lambda)}=\bm{V}\Sigma\bm{V}^{\top}
, and
\bm{W}
lies in the tangent space of
\bm{Z}^{(\lambda)}
and satisfies
\|\bm{W}\|\leq\lambda
. In addition, from the first-order necessary condition for a stationary point,
\bm{U}^{(\lambda)}
satisfies

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\bigg(\langle\bm{X}_{i},\bm{U}^{(\lambda)}\bm{U}^{(\lambda)\top}-\bm{M}\rangle/\sqrt{n}-\varepsilon_{i}\bigg)\bm{X}_{i}\bm{U}^{(\lambda)}+\lambda\bm{U}^{(\lambda)}

\displaystyle=0.

(24)

Denote

\displaystyle\bm{N}_{\varepsilon}:=\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\sum_{i}\varepsilon_{i}\bm{X}_{i}.

(25)

Equation
Eq.

24
then implies

\displaystyle\mathcal{X}^{*}\mathcal{X}(\bm{U}^{(\lambda)}\bm{U}^{(\lambda)\top}-\bm{M})-\bm{N}_{\varepsilon}

\displaystyle=-\lambda\bm{V}_{\bm{U}}\bm{V}_{\bm{U}}^{\top}+\bm{W}_{U},

(26)

where
\bm{V}_{{\bm{U}}}
is the orthonormal matrix associated to the column space of
\bm{U}^{{(\lambda)}}
, and
\bm{W}_{U}
is orthogonal to
\bm{V}_{{\bm{U}}}
. Therefore, it suffices to show that
\|\bm{W}_{U}\|<\lambda
.
We have that

\displaystyle\mathcal{X}^{*}\mathcal{X}\big(\bm{U}^{(\lambda)}\bm{U}^{(\lambda)\top}-\bm{M}\big)-\bm{N}_{\varepsilon}

\displaystyle=-\bm{M}+\bm{U}^{(\lambda)}\bm{U}^{(\lambda)\top}+\bigg(\mathcal{X}^{*}\mathcal{X}-\mathcal{I}\bigg)\big(\bm{U}^{(\lambda)}\bm{U}^{(\lambda)\top}-\bm{M}\big)-\bm{N}_{\varepsilon}.

(27)

Plugging this identity into (
26
) and rearranging yields

\displaystyle\bm{M}+\bigg(\mathcal{I}-\mathcal{X}^{*}\mathcal{X}\bigg)\big(\bm{U}^{(\lambda)}\bm{U}^{(\lambda)\top}-\bm{M}\big)+\bm{N}_{\varepsilon}

\displaystyle=\bm{U}^{(\lambda)}\bm{U}^{(\lambda)\top}+\lambda\bm{V}_{U}\bm{V}_{U}^{\top}-\bm{W}_{U}

(28)

\displaystyle=\bm{V}_{U}\bigg(\Sigma+\lambda\bm{I}\bigg)\bm{V}_{U}-\bm{W}_{U}.

(29)

Since
\bm{W}_{U}
is orthogonal to
\bm{V}_{\bm{U}}
, the eigenvalues of
\bm{W}_{U}
are equal to the bottom
d-r
eigenvalues of
\bm{V}_{U}(\Sigma+\lambda\bm{I})\bm{V}_{U}^{\top}
.
Therefore, by Weyl’s inequality, for
r<i\leq d
,

\displaystyle\sigma_{i}\bigg(\bm{V}_{U}\bigg(\Sigma+\lambda\bm{I}\bigg)\bm{V}_{U}^{\top}-\bm{W}_{U}\bigg)

\displaystyle\leq\sigma_{i}(\bm{M})+\bigg\|\bigg(\mathcal{I}-\mathcal{X}^{*}\mathcal{X}\bigg)\big(\bm{U}^{(\lambda)}\bm{U}^{(\lambda)\top}-\bm{M}\big)+\bm{N}_{\varepsilon}\bigg\|

(30)

\displaystyle\leq\bigg\|\bigg(\mathcal{I}-\mathcal{X}^{*}\mathcal{X}\bigg)\big(\bm{U}^{(\lambda)}\bm{U}^{(\lambda)\top}-\bm{M}\big)\bigg\|+\bigg\|\bm{N}_{\varepsilon}\bigg\|

(31)

\displaystyle\leq\delta_{2r}\|\bm{U}^{(\lambda)}\bm{U}^{(\lambda)\top}-\bm{M}\|_{F}+\|\bm{N}_{\varepsilon}\|

(32)

\displaystyle<\frac{\lambda}{2}+\frac{\lambda}{2}

(33)

\displaystyle<\lambda,

(34)

since by
3

\lambda
is taken to be larger than
C_{4}\sigma\sqrt{d}
, since then on
\mathcal{E}_{{\sf Good}}
it holds that
\|\bm{N}_{\varepsilon}\|\leq C\sigma\sqrt{d}<\frac{C_{4}\sigma\sqrt{d}}{2}
, and
\|\bm{U}^{(\lambda)}\bm{U}^{(\lambda)\top}-\bm{M}\|_{F}<\lambda/(2\delta_{2r})
by assumption. Thus, we have shown that
\|\bm{W}_{U}\|<\lambda
, which completes the proof.
∎

Next, note that
Lemma

2
implies
Theorem

2
if we can show that
\|\bm{U}^{(\lambda)}\bm{U}^{(\lambda)\top}-\bm{M}\|_{F}<\lambda/(2\delta_{2r})
for a local minimum
\bm{U}^{(\lambda)}
. This result is accomplished in the following lemma.

Lemma 3
.

Let
\bm{U}^{(\lambda)}
be a minimizer of
f^{(\lambda)}_{{\sf ncvx}}
satisfying the second-order necessary condition. Then on the event
\mathcal{E}_{{\sf Good}}
it holds that

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\sqrt{dr}}\|\bm{U}^{(\lambda)}\bm{U}^{(\lambda)\top}-\bm{M}\|_{F}

\displaystyle\leq C\sigma.

(35)

In particular, under
2
and
3
the conditions of
Lemma

2
hold.

Proof.

See
Section

B.1
.
∎

To prove this result we study the Hessian of
f^{(\lambda)}_{{\sf ncvx}}
evaluated at any local minimum and show that on the event
\mathcal{E}_{{\sf Good}}
the necessary conditions for a local minimum give rise to the conclusion of
Lemma

3
.

The proof of
Theorem

2
is now straightforward.

Proof of
Theorem

2
.

By
Lemma

3
, on the event
\mathcal{E}_{{\sf Good}}
, the conditions of
Lemma

2
hold. The result is proven.
∎

Control of Convex and Regularized Nonconvex Estimators

In this section we study the convex estimator
\bm{Z}^{(\lambda)}
as well as its nonconvex counterpart
\bm{U}^{(\lambda)}
, which up to the high-probability event
\mathcal{E}_{{\sf Good}}
, are equivalent in the sense of
Theorem

2
.
The main purpose of this section is to state
Theorem

4
, which demonstrates that
\phi\big(\bm{Z}^{(\lambda)})
concentrates around a soft-thresholding estimator that asymptotically approximates
\bm{Z}_{{\sf ST}}^{(\lambda)}
by
Lemma

7
stated in the following subsection. This new soft-thresholding estimator arises from our application of the Matrix CGMT that we introduce in the following subsection, the primary technical ingredient in our proof. We then give the definition of this new quantity and state
Lemma

7
in
Section

3.2
. Finally, we state
Theorem

4
and give a high-level proof in
Section

3.3
.

Matrix CGMT

The proof of
Theorem

4
uses the following generalization of the Convex Gaussian Min-Max Theorem tailored to the matrix observations that may be of independent interest. The proof is self-contained.

Theorem 3

(Matrix CGMT)
.

Let
\mathcal{A}:\mathbb{R}^{d\times d}\to\mathbb{R}^{n}
be an operator such that
\mathcal{A}(\bm{M})_{i}=\langle\bm{A}_{i},\bm{M}\rangle
, where each
\bm{A}_{i}
is a GOE matrix. Let
\bm{H}\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times d}
be a GOE matrix, and let
\bm{g}
be a standard
n
-dimensional Gaussian random vector. Let
\mathcal{S}_{\bm{W}}\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times d}
and
\mathcal{S}_{\bm{\nu}}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
be compact sets, and let
\psi:\mathcal{S}_{\bm{W}}\times\mathcal{S}_{\bm{\nu}}\to\mathbb{R}
be a continuous function. Define

\displaystyle\Phi(\mathcal{A}):

\displaystyle=\min_{\bm{W}\in\mathcal{S}_{\bm{W}}}\max_{\bm{\nu}\in\mathcal{S}_{\bm{\nu}}}\langle\mathcal{A}[\bm{W}],\bm{\nu}\rangle+\psi(\bm{W},\bm{\nu});

(36)

\displaystyle\phi(\bm{g},\bm{H}):

\displaystyle=\min_{\bm{W}\in\mathcal{S}_{\bm{W}}}\max_{\bm{\nu}\in\mathcal{S}_{\bm{\nu}}}\|\bm{W}\|_{F}\langle\bm{g},\bm{\nu}\rangle+\|\bm{\nu}\|\langle\bm{H},\bm{W}\rangle+\psi(\bm{W},\bm{\nu}).

(37)

Then it holds that

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\bigg(\Phi(\mathcal{A})\leq t\bigg)\leq 2\mathbb{P}\bigg(\phi(\bm{g,H})\leq t\bigg).

(38)

If in addition both
\mathcal{S}_{\bm{W}}
and
\mathcal{S}_{\bm{\nu}}
are convex and
\psi(\bm{W},\bm{\nu})
is convex-concave, then

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\bigg(\Phi(\mathcal{A})\geq t\bigg)\leq 2\mathbb{P}\bigg(\phi(\bm{g,H})\geq t\bigg).

(39)

Proof.

See
Section

A.5
.
∎

Observe that unlike the
vector
CGMT
(
Thrampoulidis et al. 2015a
)
, the operator
\mathcal{A}
consists of
{\sf GOE}(d)
matrices, whereas the vector CGMT uses a single random matrix, and the minimization is over the bilinear form generated by this random matrix. In
Theorem

3
the new quantity
\phi(\bm{g},\bm{H})
converts the randomness in
\mathcal{A}
to two independent sources of randomness: the random vector
\bm{g}
and the random matrix
\bm{H}
. In contrast, the vector CGMT converts the randomness in the single random matrix into two independent random Gaussian vectors.

A Family of Soft-Thresholding Estimators and Associated Fixed-Point Equations

In this subsection we give the definition of the particular soft-thresholding estimator about which
\bm{Z}^{(\lambda)}
concentrates in
Theorem

4
. We will also state
Lemma

7
, which shows that this soft-thresholding estimator is asymptotically equal to
\bm{Z}_{{\sf ST}}^{(\lambda)}
. En route to stating this result, we state several intermediate results that we rely on throughout our proofs.

For two scalars
\tau,\zeta
, we define the soft-thresholding estimator

\displaystyle\bm{Z}_{{\sf ST}}^{(\lambda/\zeta)}(\tau)

\displaystyle=\argmin_{\bm{Z}\succcurlyeq 0}\frac{\zeta}{2}\|\bm{Z}-(\bm{M}+\tau\bm{H})\|_{F}^{2}+\lambda\|\bm{Z}\|_{*}.

(40)

In
Theorem

4
we show that
\bm{Z}^{(\lambda)}
approximates
\bm{Z}^{(\lambda/\zeta^{*})}(\tau^{*})
for appropriate choices of
(\tau^{*},\zeta^{*})
that are asymptotically equal to
(\sigma,1)
. In fact, observe that by taking
\zeta=1
and
\tau=\sigma
above we arrive at
\bm{Z}_{{\sf ST}}^{(\lambda)}
as in
Theorem

1
.

The pair (
\tau^{*},\zeta^{*}
) arise directly from our analysis via the Matrix CGMT. Here we give a heuristic derivation, with additional details in
Section

A.3.3
. Recall that
\bm{Z}^{(\lambda)}
satisfies

\displaystyle\bm{Z}^{(\lambda)}=\argmin_{\bm{Z}\succcurlyeq 0}\frac{1}{2}\|\mathcal{X}(\bm{Z-M})-\bm{\varepsilon}\|_{F}^{2}+\lambda\|\bm{Z}\|_{*}.

(41)

By reparameterizing this cost function with
\bm{W}:=\bm{Z}-\bm{M}
and expanding out the Frobenius norm in its variational form, we arrive at the new cost function

\displaystyle C_{\lambda}^{(n)}(\bm{W})

\displaystyle=\max_{\bm{\nu}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}}\langle\mathcal{X}[\bm{W}],\bm{\nu}\rangle-\langle\bm{\varepsilon},\bm{\nu}\rangle-\frac{1}{2}\|\bm{\nu}\|^{2}+\lambda\big\{\|\bm{W+M}\|_{*}-\|\bm{M}\|_{*}\big\}.

(42)

Consequently, since we are minimizing over
\bm{W}
, the minimization above takes the form required for the Matrix CGMT. Therefore,
we can show that the minimum value of
C_{\lambda}^{(n)}
over any set
\mathcal{W}
is related to that of the function
L_{\lambda}^{(n)}(\mathcal{W})
, defined via

\displaystyle L_{\lambda}^{(n)}(\mathcal{W})

\displaystyle=\max_{\beta>0}\min_{\tau\geq\sigma}\bigg(\frac{\sigma^{2}}{\tau}+\tau\bigg)\frac{\|\bm{g}\|}{\sqrt{n}}\frac{\gamma_{n}\beta}{2}-\gamma_{n}\frac{\beta^{2}}{2}

(43a)

\displaystyle\quad+\min_{\begin{subarray}{c}\bm{W}\in\mathcal{W}\\
\bm{W}+\bm{M}\succcurlyeq 0\end{subarray}}\bigg\{\beta\frac{\|\bm{W}\|_{F}^{2}}{2dr\tau}+\beta\frac{\langle\bm{H},\bm{W}\rangle}{dr}+\frac{\lambda}{dr}\big\{\|\bm{W}+\bm{M}\|_{*}-\|\bm{M}\|_{*}\big\}\bigg\}.

(43b)

Note that the argument of
L_{\lambda}^{(n)}
is a set (the set for which
\bm{W}
is minimized over). We will also abuse notation and denote
L_{\lambda}(\bm{W})=L_{\lambda}(\{\bm{W}\})
.

It turns out that
L_{\lambda}^{(n)}(\mathbb{R}^{d\times d})
concentrates about the quantity
L_{\lambda}^{*}
(roughly speaking, its expected value) defined via

\displaystyle L_{\lambda}^{*}:=\max_{\beta>0}\min_{\tau\geq\sigma}\psi_{\lambda}^{*}(\tau,\beta),

(44)

where
\psi^{*}_{\lambda}(\cdot,\cdot)
is the (scalar) valued function defined via

\displaystyle\psi^{*}_{\lambda}(\tau,\beta)

\displaystyle:=\bigg(\frac{\sigma^{2}}{\tau}+\tau\bigg)\frac{\gamma_{n}\beta}{2}-\gamma_{n}\frac{\beta^{2}}{2}+\mathbb{E}\min_{\bm{Z}}\beta\frac{\|\bm{Z}-\bm{M}\|_{F}^{2}}{2dr\tau}+\beta\frac{\langle\bm{H},\bm{Z}-\bm{M}\rangle}{dr}+\frac{\lambda}{dr}\big(\|\bm{Z}\|_{*}-\|\bm{M}\|_{*}\big).

(45)

Note that
\psi^{*}(\tau,\beta)
resembles (
43
).
From this derivation, we arrive at the definition of
(\tau^{*},\zeta^{*})
. We define
(\tau^{*},\zeta^{*})
such that

\displaystyle L_{\lambda}^{*}

\displaystyle=\psi_{\lambda}^{*}(\tau^{*},\zeta^{*}/\tau^{*}).

In words, the quantities
\tau^{*}
and
\beta^{*}=\zeta^{*}/\tau^{*}
are simply the critical points such that
L_{\lambda}^{*}=\psi^{*}_{\lambda}(\tau^{*},\beta^{*})
.

It is not immediate that
(\tau^{*},\zeta^{*})
are well-defined. To establish existence and uniqueness, first we give the following lemma that gives an alternative characterization in terms of of fixed-point equations which can be obtained by studying the first-order necessary conditions for the critical points of
\psi_{\lambda}^{*}
.

Lemma 4
.

Define the system of equations

\displaystyle\tau^{2}

\displaystyle=\sigma^{2}+\frac{dr}{n}{\sf R}_{\lambda}\big(\tau^{2},\zeta);

(46a)

\displaystyle\zeta

\displaystyle=1-\frac{{\sf df}_{\lambda}\big(\tau^{2},\zeta)}{n},

(46b)

where

\displaystyle{\sf R}_{\lambda}(\tau^{2},\zeta):

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{dr}\mathbb{E}\bigg[\big\|\bm{Z}_{\sf ST}^{(\lambda/\zeta)}(\tau)-\bm{M}\|_{F}^{2}\bigg];

(47)

\displaystyle{\sf df}_{\lambda}(\tau^{2},\zeta)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\tau}\mathbb{E}\bigg\langle\bm{H},\bm{Z}_{\sf ST}^{(\lambda/\zeta)}(\tau)\bigg\rangle

(48)

where the expectation is with respect to
\bm{H}\sim{\sf GOE}(d)
. Let
\tau^{*}
and
\beta^{*}=\zeta^{*}\tau^{*}
be such that
(\tau^{*},\zeta^{*})
satisfy the system of equations (
46
). Then it holds that
L^{*}_{\lambda}=\psi^{*}_{\lambda}(\tau^{*},\beta^{*})
.

Proof.

Simply take the derivative and set equal to zero.
∎

Readers familiar with the literature on proportional asymptotics for linear regression may recognize that the fixed-point equations (
46
) bear significant similarity to other fixed-point equations that arise in the study of high-dimensional (regularized) M-estimators
(
Bellec and Koriyama 2024
;
Donoho and Montanari 2016
;
El Karoui et al. 2013
;
Koriyama and Bellec 2025
;
Berthier et al. 2020
)
. In particular, the equations (
46
) have an interpretation as the matrix analogue of the fixed-point equations for the LASSO studied in
Celentano et al. 2023
and
Miolane and Montanari 2021
. For more details, see the explanation in
Celentano et al. 2023
.

Next, the following lemma relies on the system of equations (
46
) to establish the existence and uniqueness of
(\tau^{*},\zeta^{*})
.

Lemma 5

(Existence and Uniqueness of
(\tau^{*},\zeta^{*})
)
.

For any fixed
\lambda>0
, if
n,d\geq C
, there exist unique
\zeta^{*}>0
and
\tau^{*}\geq\sigma
that satisfy the system of equations (
46
).

Proof.

See
Section

A.2
.
∎

Thus far we have shown that
\bm{Z}^{(\lambda/\zeta^{*})}(\tau^{*})
, which is more directly related to
\bm{Z}^{(\lambda)}
, is well-defined. However, in
Theorem

1
we show that
\bm{Z}^{(\lambda)}
concentrates around
\bm{Z}^{(\lambda)}_{{\sf ST}}\equiv\bm{Z}^{(\lambda)}_{{\sf ST}}(\sigma)
. Therefore, the following result shows that under
1
,
2
and
3
that
\tau^{*}
and
\zeta^{*}
approximate
\sigma
and
1
respectively.

Lemma 6

(Properties of the Fixed-Point Solutions)
.

Suppose that
1
,
2
and
3
are satisfied. Then it holds that
\sigma\leq\tau^{*}\leq C\sigma
and

\displaystyle|(\tau^{*})^{2}-\sigma^{2}|=O\bigg(\frac{dr}{n}\sigma^{2}\bigg);\qquad|\zeta^{*}-1|=O\bigg(\frac{dr^{3/2}}{n}\bigg),

(49)

where the implicit constants depend on the constants in our assumptions.

Proof.

See
Section

A.3
.
∎

In
Section

A.3
we also establish several key properties of the soft-thresholding estimator
\bm{Z}_{{\sf ST}}^{(\lambda/\zeta^{*})}(\tau^{*})
that we use throughout our proofs. Finally, the following lemma establishes that the replacement of
\bm{Z}_{{\sf ST}}^{(\lambda/\zeta^{*})}(\tau^{*})
with
\bm{Z}_{{\sf ST}}^{(\lambda)}
is asymptotically negligible.

Lemma 7
.

Suppose that
1
,
2
and
3
are satisfied. Then for any
1
-Lipschitz function
\phi:\mathbb{R}^{d\times d}\to\mathbb{R}
it holds that

\displaystyle\bigg|\mathbb{E}\phi\big(\bm{Z}_{{\sf ST}}^{(\lambda/\zeta^{*})}(\tau^{*})/\sqrt{dr}\big)-\mathbb{E}\phi\big(\bm{Z}_{{\sf ST}}^{(\lambda)}/\sqrt{dr}\big)\bigg|=O\bigg(\sigma\frac{dr^{3/2}}{n}\bigg).

(50)

Proof.

See
Section

A.4
.
∎

High-Dimensional Properties of Convex and Regularized Nonconvex Estimators

We are now prepared to establish the high-dimensional properties of the convex estimator and its nonconvex counterpart for the choice
\tau^{*}
and
\zeta^{*}
from the previous section. The full proof of this result is in
Appendix

C
, though we give a high-level sketch of the proof momentarily.

Theorem 4
.

Suppose that
1
,
2
and
3
are satisfied. Then for any 1-Lipschitz function
\phi:\mathbb{R}^{d\times d}\to\mathbb{R}
and for any
\varepsilon
satisfying
\varepsilon>C\exp(-cdr)
, it holds that

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\bigg\{

\displaystyle\bigg|\phi\bigg(\frac{1}{\sqrt{dr}}\bm{U}^{(\lambda)}\bm{U}{(\lambda)\top}\bigg)-\mathbb{E}_{\bm{H}}\phi\bigg(\frac{1}{\sqrt{dr}}\bm{Z}^{(\lambda/\zeta^{*})}_{{\sf ST}}(\tau^{*})\bigg)\bigg|>\varepsilon\bigg\}

(51)

\displaystyle\leq O\bigg(\exp(-cd)+\exp(-cdr)+\exp(-cn)+\frac{1}{\varepsilon^{2}}\exp(-cdr\varepsilon^{4})-\exp(-\frac{(dr)^{2}}{n}\varepsilon^{4})\bigg),

(52)

where the constants depend on problem parameters. The same result holds if
\bm{U}^{(\lambda)}\bm{U}^{(\lambda)\top}
is replaced with
\bm{Z}^{(\lambda)}
.

We remark that
Theorem

4
does not contain an additional error term depending on
\gamma_{n}
. In essence, this is due to the fact that we replace
\bm{Z}_{{\sf ST}}^{(\lambda/\zeta^{*})}(\tau^{*})
with
\bm{Z}_{{\sf ST}}^{(\lambda)}=\bm{Z}_{{\sf ST}}^{(\lambda)}(\sigma)
in the statement of
Theorem

1
. As shown in
Lemma

6
, it holds that
\tau^{*}=\sigma+O(\gamma_{n}^{-1})
when
\gamma_{n}
is large. Therefore, the additional error term in
Theorem

1
arises due to this replacement. In the asymptotic regime
\frac{n}{dr^{2}}\asymp 1
, this replacement is non-negligible, but
Theorem

4
ameliorates this.

Theorem

4
is comparable to the results on the LASSO developed in
Miolane and Montanari 2021
;
Celentano et al. 2023
;
Javanmard and Montanari 2018
, among others. The statement is most directly related to the main result of
Celentano et al. 2023
, who establish an equivalence between the LASSO estimator for sparse linear regression with the corresponding soft-thresholding of a vector denoising problem. The primary difference between our result and theirs comes from the fact that we are considering matrices as opposed to vectors, whence the function
L_{\lambda}^{(n)}
is no longer strongly convex. One of our major contributions and key technical ingredients is to show that
L_{\lambda}^{(n)}
maintains strong convexity when restricted to rank at most
r
matrices.

Sketch of the Proof of
Theorem

4
.

The high-level strategy will be to argue that any minimizer of
C_{\lambda}^{(n)}(\bm{W})
over the shifted positive semidefinite cone
\{\bm{W}:\bm{W}+\bm{M}\succcurlyeq 0\}
must be within
\varepsilon\sqrt{dr}
of
\bm{Z}_{{\sf ST}}^{(\lambda/\zeta^{*})}(\tau^{*})
with high probability.
Let
\bm{\widehat{W}}:=\bm{Z}_{{\sf ST}}^{(\lambda/\zeta^{*})}(\tau^{*})-\bm{M}
, and let
\phi
be a fixed 1-Lipschitz function. Consider the set
\mathcal{D}
defined via

\displaystyle\mathcal{D}:=\bigg\{\bm{W}:\bigg|\phi\bigg(\frac{\bm{W}+\bm{M}}{\sqrt{dr}}\bigg)-\mathbb{E}\phi\bigg(\frac{\bm{\widehat{W}}+\bm{M}}{\sqrt{dr}}\bigg)\bigg|\leq\frac{\varepsilon}{2}\bigg\}\cap\bigg\{\bm{W}+\bm{M}\succcurlyeq 0\bigg\},

(53)

and let
\mathcal{D}_{\varepsilon}
denote the
\varepsilon
-enlargement of the set
\mathcal{D}
; i.e.
\mathcal{D}_{\varepsilon}:=\{\bm{V}=\bm{W}+\bm{W}^{\prime}:\|\bm{W}^{\prime}\|_{F}\leq\varepsilon,\bm{W}\in\mathcal{D}\}
. Since by the proof of
Theorem

2

\bm{Z}^{(\lambda)}
is rank at most
r
and within radius
R=C\sigma\sqrt{dr}
of
\bm{M}
with high probability, we will show that any rank at most
r
minimum of
C^{(n)}_{\lambda}
on
\mathcal{D}_{\varepsilon/2}^{c}
(outside of the
\varepsilon/2
ball) but within radius
R
of
\bm{M}
must be suboptimal with high probability, which will imply that the minimizer must lie within
\mathcal{D}_{\varepsilon/2}
with high probability.

Carrying out this argument, in our full proof of
Theorem

4
in
Appendix

C
, we show via the Matrix CGMT that it holds that

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\bigg\{\argmin~

\displaystyle C^{(n)}_{\lambda}(\bm{W})\notin\mathcal{D}_{\varepsilon/2}\bigg\}

(54)

\displaystyle\leq 2\mathbb{P}\bigg\{\bm{\widehat{W}}\notin\mathcal{D}\bigg\}+2\mathbb{P}\bigg\{L_{\lambda}^{(n)}(\mathbb{R}^{d\times d})\geq L_{\lambda}^{*}+C\varepsilon^{2}\bigg\}

(55)

\displaystyle+2\mathbb{P}\bigg\{L_{\lambda}^{(n)}\bigg(\mathbb{B}_{\varepsilon/2}^{c}(\bm{\widehat{W}}/\sqrt{dr})\cap\{\mathrm{rank}(\bm{W+M})\leq r\}\cap\{\|\bm{W}\|_{F}\leq R\}\bigg)\leq L_{\lambda}^{*}+C\varepsilon^{2}\bigg\},

(56)

where
L_{\lambda}^{*}
is the quantity defined in the previous subsection. Therefore, the proof boils down to analyzing the three terms on the right hand side above. It turns out that the first two terms are straightforward to bound: the first term is exponentially small as
\bm{Z}^{(\lambda/\zeta^{*})}_{{\sf ST}}(\tau^{*})
is a proximal operator and hence Lipschitz. The second term is also well-behaved as
L_{\lambda}^{(n)}
is expected to concentrate about
L_{\lambda}^{*}
at its global minimum.

The final remaining quantity is significantly more difficult to analyze. The term inside the probability can be understood as the minimum of value of
L_{\lambda}^{(n)}
over the set of matrices
\bm{W}
such that:

1.

They are at least
\varepsilon/2
distance away from
\bm{\widehat{W}}
;

2.

\bm{W}+\bm{M}
is rank at most
r
;

3.

\bm{W}
has norm at most
R
.

In order to analyze the function
L_{\lambda}^{(n)}
over matrices such that
\mathrm{rank}(\bm{W}+\bm{M})\leq r
, we define the auxiliary function

\displaystyle h(\bm{U})

\displaystyle=\frac{\gamma_{n}}{2}\bigg(\sqrt{\frac{\|\bm{UU}^{\top}-\bm{M}\|_{F}^{2}}{n}+\sigma^{2}}\frac{\|\bm{g}\|}{\sqrt{n}}-\frac{\langle\bm{H},\bm{UU}^{\top}-\bm{M}\rangle}{n}\bigg)_{+}^{2}+\frac{\lambda}{dr}\big\{\|\bm{U}\|_{F}^{2}-\|\bm{M}\|_{*}\big\}.

(57)

It is straightforward to see that if
\bm{W}=\bm{UU}^{\top}-\bm{M}
, then
h(\bm{U})=L_{\lambda}^{(n)}(\bm{W})
. Therefore, analyzing
L_{\lambda}^{(n)}
over rank at most
r
matrices is equivalent to analyzing the function
h(\bm{U})
over
\mathbb{R}^{d\times r}
. Intuitively, since
L_{\lambda}^{(n)}(\mathbb{R}^{d\times d})
is close to
L_{\lambda}^{*}
, the optimization geometry of
L_{\lambda}^{(n)}
should be similar to that of matrix soft-thresholding. Therefore, since
h(\bm{U})
is the “nonconvex version” of
L_{\lambda}^{(n)}
, the geometry of
h(\bm{U})
should behave similarly to that of nonconvex matrix factorization.

Carrying out this intuition, in
Lemma

16
we show that the function
h(\bm{U})
is strongly convex on an appropriate high-probability event
\mathcal{E}_{h}
within a region
\mathcal{R}\subset\mathbb{R}^{d\times r}
defined via

\displaystyle\mathcal{R}:=\bigg\{\bm{U}:\|\bm{U}-\bm{U}^{*}\mathcal{O}_{\bm{U}^{*},\bm{U}}\|\leq\frac{c}{\kappa}\|\bm{U}^{*}\|\bigg\},

(58)

where
c
is some universal constant,
\bm{U}^{*}
is such that
\bm{M}=\bm{U}^{*}\bm{U}^{*\top}
, and
\mathcal{O}_{\bm{U}^{*},\bm{U}}
is the Frobenius-optimal orthogonal matrix aligning
\bm{U}
and
\bm{U}^{*}
. This argument is significantly different from previous analyses for the LASSO, in which the analogue of
L_{\lambda}^{(n)}
is already strongly convex in a region near its optimal value
(
Miolane and Montanari 2021
)
. In contrast, in our setting the function
L_{\lambda}^{(n)}
need not be strongly convex near
\bm{\widehat{W}}
, but
h(\bm{U})
can be shown to be (near
\bm{Z}_{{\sf ST}}^{(\lambda/\zeta^{*})}(\tau^{*})
). This discrepancy is due to the fact that
\langle\bm{H},\bm{W}\rangle
does not concentrate at rate
O(\sqrt{dr}\|\bm{W}\|_{F})
uniformly over all matrices
\bm{W}
